" style="padding: 16px 0;"><div class="ayah-card" id="ayah-card-14-34" data-asset-id="asset-ayah-card-1"><div class="golden-surah-header clickable-ayah-header" data-ayats="34" data-original-token="Q|14:34" data-surah="14" id="ayah-header-14-34"><span>Abraham (14:34)</span></div><p class="ayah-arabic">‏ ‏وَآتَىٰكُم مّ
ن كُلّ مَا سَأَلْتُمُوهُ وَإن تَعُدُّوا۟ نعْمَتَ ٱللَّه لَا تُحْصُوهَا إنَّ ٱلْإنسَنَ لَظَلُومٌۭ كَفَّارٌۭ ‎ <img class="ayah-number-img fr-fic fr-dii" src="ht
tps://myislam.sfo3.digitaloceanspaces.com/assets/2024/03/ayat/ayah-34.png" alt="34">&nbsp;‎</p><div class="translation-header">Translation:.</div><p class="aya
h-translation">And he gave you from all you asked of him. And if you should count the favor of Allah, you could not enumerate them. Indeed, mankind is [generally] most unjust and ungrateful.&nbsp;</p></div></div><p>Indeed man is extremely unjust and an ingrate. The word <span class="inlineArabic">كَفَّار</span> in th
is verse is not used in the meaning of "disbeliever," but as "ingrate." The sending of messengers is rooted in Allah’s profound good intention toward His creation. If Allah had simply created humanity and withheld the manual for life, we would all be left to wander in a state of turmoil and darkness, clueless about our purpose.</p><p>This state of aimless wandering is reminiscent of the stories of Mulla Nasruddin. In one tale, Mulla Nasruddin is wandering the streets in the early morning hours. When a policeman asks him what he is doing, he replies, "If I knew the answer to that, I'd be at home in bed." This reflects the reality of many people who wander through life in a stupor, constantly seeking distractions and entertainment to avoid the pain of existence. The great scholar Rumi speaks of the many "wines" Allah has put on Earth to numb people's misery. He notes that Jesus (AS) was "drunk in the love of Allah, while his donkey was high on barley."</p><p>This attitude of "whatever gets you through the night," as John Lennon said, is a form of ingratitude from the Islamic perspective. The messengers have already brought the news and guidance, yet people refuse to accept it. It is like being thirsty and given directions to a river, but choosing instead to watch a show or play a video game to forget the thirst. This is ingratitude.</p><p>Another story of Mulla Nasruddin illustrates a similar point: he was searching for his keys under a streetlight, and when someone asked him where he lost them, he pointed to a dark area away from the light. When asked why he was looking where he was, he replied, "The light is better over here, right?" This highlights another type of truth concealment: going where it is easy instead of where it is hard. This is where we begin to dilute religion to make our lives comfortable. Messengers, as human beings, set an incredible precedent by following 
Allah's Book to the letter, proving that what is decreed is humanly possible to achieve.</p>, Truth Concealment as Ingratitude ], StreamIds: [  ] }. {"EventId": {"Id": 1, "Name": "ReceivedHubInvocation"}, "TransportConnectionId": "-pg6VdEC5QhXm4sqTGi7Gg", "RequestId": "0HNHBJCMA27TM:00000001", "RequestPath": "/hub/session", "ConnectionId": "0HNHBJCMA27TM"}
